File Contents:
===== .github/workflows/deploy.yml =====
name: Deploy to GitHub Pages

on:
  push:
    branches:
      - main
  workflow_dispatch:

# Allow GITHUB_TOKEN to publish to Pages and verify the deployment origin.
permissions:
  contents: read
  pages: write
  id-token: write

# One deployment at a time; let an in-flight run finish rather than cancelling it.
concurrency:
  group: pages
  cancel-in-progress: false

jobs:
  build:
    name: Build
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - name: Checkout
        uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - name: Set up Node
        uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with:
          node-version: 22
          cache: npm

      - name: Install dependencies
        run: npm ci

      - name: Validate answer bank
        run: npm run validate

      - name: Type-check and build
        run: npm run build

      - name: Configure Pages
        uses: actions/configure-pages@v5

      - name: Upload build artifact
        uses: actions/upload-pages-artifact@v3
        with:
          path: dist

  deploy:
    name: Deploy
    needs: build
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    environment:
      name: github-pages
      url: ${{ steps.deployment.outputs.page_url }}
    steps:
      - name: Deploy to GitHub Pages
        id: deployment
        uses: actions/deploy-pages@v4

===== opencode-task.md =====
# Task: Upgrade the Existing Krillion-Style Trivia Game

Act as a senior frontend engineer, game designer, and UI/UX designer. Improve my existing trivia game into a polished, endlessly replayable, rarest-answer trivia experience inspired by Krillion. You have a global frontend skill that can help with the UX/UI design. Use that religously.

Do not merely provide recommendations or a plan. Inspect the existing repository, implement the changes directly, run the available validation and build commands, and fix any issues caused by your changes.

## 1. Start by understanding the codebase

Before editing anything:

- Inspect the project structure, `package.json`, TypeScript configuration, game engine, scoring system, prompt bank, UI components, and all relevant CSS files.
- Understand how the existing game modes, endless mode, answer grading, rarity tiers, scoring, timers, persistence, statistics, animations, and audio work.
- Identify existing functionality that must be preserved.
- Use the current architecture and conventions wherever practical.
- Do not rewrite the entire application or introduce a new framework.
- Do not remove features simply because they complicate the implementation.
- Avoid unnecessary dependencies. Prefer lightweight, dependency-free TypeScript solutions where possible.

Create a short implementation plan, then execute it without stopping for approval unless a genuinely blocking decision arises.

## 2. Redesign the entire visual theme

The current colour scheme is too colourful. Replace it with a darker, more minimal, sophisticated aesthetic.

### Design direction

Think premium dark-mode gaming interface, understated editorial typography, subtle industrial styling, and restrained crimson-red highlights. The interface should feel deliberate and atmospheric rather than cartoonish, excessively animated, or overly decorative.

Use a consistent design-token system with CSS custom properties.

Suggested palette:

- Main background: `#090909`
- Secondary background: `#101010`
- Card background: `#151515`
- Elevated surface: `#1B1B1B`
- Borders and dividers: `#292929`
- Primary text: `#F2F2F2`
- Secondary text: `#A0A0A0`
- Muted text: `#686868`
- Primary red accent: `#E53945`
- Dark red accent: `#8F1D28`
- Subtle red surface: `#251114`

Treat these as starting points. Adjust shades when needed to maintain accessibility and visual consistency.

### Apply the theme throughout the application

Redesign all existing screens and states, including:

- Home screen and game logo.
- Game mode selection cards.
- Endless mode and active question screen.
- Answer input and autocomplete dropdown.
- Countdown timer, air/lives indicator, and progress indicators.
- Rarity tier legend and scoring feedback.
- Accepted and rejected answer states.
- Hints and feedback messages.
- Results screen, statistics, and replay controls.
- Saved-run and resume states.
- Buttons, hover states, focus states, loading states, and empty states.

### Specific visual requirements

- Use black and charcoal as the dominant colours.
- Reserve red for meaningful emphasis: primary actions, active states, timer urgency, important scores, and selected rarity highlights.
- Use subtle borders rather than bright outlines or excessive shadows.
- Remove unnecessary gradients, bright blue/cyan colours, excessive glow effects, and decorative visual clutter.
- Keep typography clean and readable. Use a restrained type scale, clear hierarchy, and consistent spacing.
- Make cards and buttons feel crisp, compact, and intentional.
- Use smooth, short transitions rather than flashy animations.
- Keep the interface legible on small screens and at desktop sizes.
- Maintain visible keyboard focus indicators and sufficient contrast.
- Preserve the game's existing identity and distinctive underwater/depth theme where it adds value, but interpret it through the new minimal aesthetic. Avoid making the whole interface look like a generic ocean website.
- Remove or tone down existing background effects if they compete with the content.

The result should look cohesive, modern, dark, and minimal, with red providing the visual identity.

## 3. Implement intelligent answer autocomplete

This is one of the most important features.

When a player starts typing an answer, show a compact, responsive autocomplete dropdown directly below the input.

### Autocomplete behaviour

- Update suggestions immediately as the user types.
- Support exact prefixes, partial words, and reasonable spelling variations.
- Match answers case-insensitively.
- Handle extra spaces, missing spaces, punctuation, hyphens, apostrophes, and common diacritic variations.
- Support multiword answers even when the player only types part of the answer.
- Support common abbreviations, nicknames, alternate spellings, and aliases where appropriate.
- Rank the closest and most relevant matches first.
- Limit the dropdown to a small number of suggestions, such as five, to keep the interface clean.
- Highlight the matching portion of each suggestion.
- Allow mouse selection and keyboard navigation using the arrow keys, Enter, and Escape.
- Ensure Enter selects a highlighted suggestion or submits the answer when appropriate.
- Prevent keyboard handling from accidentally submitting the same answer twice.
- Handle empty inputs and inputs with no suggestions gracefully.
- Keep the dropdown inside the viewport on mobile and prevent it from being clipped by surrounding containers.
- Provide an accessible implementation with appropriate combobox/listbox semantics and keyboard focus management.

### Important gameplay fairness

Autocomplete should help players express the answer they already have in mind, not turn the game into a list of answers to browse.

- Do not display the complete answer bank when the input is empty.
- Do not reveal rarity tiers, scores, or other hidden answer metadata in suggestions.
- Require meaningful input before showing suggestions.
- Use a strict prefix match or a strong similarity threshold before displaying fuzzy matches.
- Avoid suggesting unrelated answers just because they share a few characters.
- Keep the suggestion count limited.

### Use the existing answer data

Integrate autocomplete with the existing prompt bank and scoring system. Do not create an unrelated, hardcoded autocomplete dictionary.

Suggestions must be drawn from answers accepted for the current prompt, with aliases handled appropriately. Never suggest answers that belong exclusively to unrelated prompts.

If useful, extend the existing answer data structure to support additional aliases, but preserve compatibility with the current prompt bank.

### Improve answer matching

Inspect the current answer-normalisation and grading implementation before making changes.

Implement a robust, deterministic matching pipeline with the following order of preference:

1. Exact normalised answer match.
2. Exact alias match.
3. Safe normalisation of punctuation, spacing, diacritics, and common formatting differences.
4. Conservative fuzzy matching for genuine spelling mistakes.

Use an appropriate edit-distance or similarity algorithm, such as bounded Levenshtein distance, with sensible length-dependent thresholds.

Requirements:

- A small typo in a long answer should generally be accepted when the intended answer is unambiguous.
- Short answers must have stricter matching rules to avoid accidental matches.
- Do not accept answers based only on a weak substring match.
- Do not let fuzzy matching map an answer to an unrelated accepted answer.
- If multiple candidates have similar similarity scores, do not arbitrarily accept the wrong one.
- Preserve the canonical answer so scoring always uses the correct rarity tier.
- Preserve duplicate-answer detection and existing run-level repeat handling.
- Do not allow autocomplete or fuzzy matching to change the intended rarity tier.
- Do not let an incorrect spelling receive a higher score than the canonical answer it resolves to.
- Keep the scoring engine deterministic and testable.

Where ambiguity remains, prefer rejecting the answer over silently awarding the wrong tier.

## 4. Expand the prompt bank and improve question variety

The game becomes repetitive if the same categories appear too often. Expand and improve the existing prompt bank substantially.

Aim for at least 100 distinct, high-quality prompts overall, preferably more if you can maintain accuracy. First count the existing unique prompts and add enough to reach the target.

### Broaden the category coverage

Include a balanced mixture of topics such as:

- Nature, animals, birds, insects, plants, and marine life.
- Geography, countries, cities, landmarks, rivers, mountains, and islands.
- Science, chemistry, physics, astronomy, anatomy, and biology.
- History, ancient civilisations, historical figures, and events.
- Food, cuisines, ingredients, spices, desserts, and cooking.
- Sport, sporting events, athletes, equipment, and terminology.
- Films, television, books, authors, fictional characters, and franchises.
- Music, genres, instruments, artists, and music theory.
- Technology, computing, programming, and video games.
- Language, literature, mythology, art, and architecture.
- Brands, products, transport, cars, and engineering.
- Everyday knowledge, hobbies, board games, and unusual specialist subjects.

Include a good balance of general knowledge, pop culture, specialist knowledge, and unexpected categories. Avoid overloading the game with obscure scientific names or geography questions.

### Prompt quality

Every new prompt must:

- Have a clear, unambiguous question.
- Have a useful category and concise hint.
- Have answers that genuinely satisfy the question.
- Include multiple valid answers across the existing rarity tiers.
- Have appropriate common answers and genuinely less common alternatives.
- Include useful aliases for common alternate spellings, abbreviations, and names.
- Avoid duplicate answers across tiers within the same prompt.
- Avoid invented, misspelled, outdated, or incorrectly classified answers.
- Avoid answers that are technically unrelated to the prompt.
- Avoid treating a different category, an unrelated entity, or a dubious answer as correct simply to fill a rarity tier.

Prefer verified, defensible answers over sheer quantity. If a prompt cannot support six meaningful rarity tiers, redesign the prompt or use fewer tiers only if the existing game architecture explicitly supports that safely. Do not populate rare tiers with fabricated answers merely to reach a target.

### Improve prompt selection

Inspect the current randomisation and endless-mode selection logic.

- Reduce the chance of seeing the same prompt repeatedly within a short period.
- Avoid immediately repeating the previous question.
- Maintain a sensible balance of categories over a run.
- Ensure endless mode can continue indefinitely without exhausting a fixed queue.
- Avoid predictable, repetitive sequences.
- Preserve deterministic daily runs and their existing replay behaviour.
- Ensure saved runs resume correctly and do not unexpectedly change their current prompt.
- Keep prompt selection compatible with the existing random-number generator and seeded modes.

Use the existing category metadata or extend it if needed. Avoid adding a complicated scheduling system when a lightweight solution is sufficient.

## 5. Improve the endless experience

Endless mode should feel like a complete, replayable game mode rather than a short mode extended indefinitely.

- Ensure prompts continue to appear until the player loses or chooses to stop.
- Keep difficulty and category variety interesting over long sessions.
- Preserve the existing scoring, depth progression, timers, hints, and air/lives mechanics unless a change is necessary to fix a bug.
- Ensure a failed answer, timeout, accepted answer, repeat answer, hint, and transition to the next question all behave correctly.
- Provide clear, understated visual feedback for each result.
- Make the current score, depth, remaining air/lives, and timer easy to understand at a glance.
- Keep the answer field as the primary interaction.
- Avoid modal dialogs that unnecessarily interrupt gameplay.
- Ensure restarting and returning home work reliably.
- Preserve local persistence and existing statistics.

Do not make endless mode easier by awarding points for incorrect answers. The rarity-based scoring concept must remain intact.

## 6. Performance, accessibility, and responsive design

- Avoid unnecessary dependencies and large bundles.
- Do not run an expensive fuzzy search over the entire prompt bank on every keystroke. Search only the active prompt's answer candidates and use sensible limits.
- Debounce only if genuinely necessary; ordinary local matching should feel immediate.
- Clean up event listeners and timers correctly.
- Preserve mobile usability, desktop usability, and keyboard-only operation.
- Use semantic HTML and accessible labels.
- Respect reduced-motion preferences.
- Ensure autocomplete does not obscure important controls or become unusable on touchscreens.
- Ensure the dark theme has adequate contrast and clear focus indicators.
- Do not introduce external APIs, backend services, accounts, analytics, or tracking.

## 7. Testing and validation

Add or update tests for the important game logic, particularly:

- Exact answer matches.
- Aliases and alternative spellings.
- Missing spaces, punctuation, and case differences.
- Diacritic normalisation.
- Short and long spelling mistakes.
- False-positive fuzzy matches.
- Ambiguous candidates.
- Correct rarity-tier assignment.
- Duplicate-answer handling.
- Autocomplete relevance, ranking, and result limits.
- Endless-mode prompt variety and repeat avoidance.
- Daily determinism and saved-run compatibility.
- Prompt-bank data integrity and duplicate detection.

Use the project's existing testing setup if one exists. If no test framework exists, prefer lightweight tests for the core pure functions rather than installing a large testing stack unnecessarily.

Run the available validation and build commands, including:

- `npm run validate`
- `npm run typecheck`
- `npm run build`

Fix any failures caused by the changes. Do not claim that a command passed unless you actually ran it and checked the result.

## 8. Implementation rules

- Make real changes to the repository, not just a mock-up.
- Keep the TypeScript types accurate and avoid introducing `any` unnecessarily.
- Keep scoring and game logic separate from UI presentation.
- Reuse existing components and utilities when appropriate.
- Avoid duplicating normalisation or grading logic in multiple places.
- Preserve existing functionality and backward compatibility with saved local data.
- Do not replace valid existing prompts with fabricated data.
- Do not silently swallow errors.
- Do not modify unrelated configuration or files.
- Do not overwrite or discard existing user changes.
- Review the final diff to ensure the implementation is coherent.

## 9. Final deliverable

When finished, provide a concise summary covering:

1. The visual redesign and the principal colours used.
2. The autocomplete and spelling-tolerance implementation.
3. The number of unique prompts before and after the change.
4. The main improvements to prompt diversity and endless mode.
5. The files and systems changed.
6. The tests, validation commands, and build results.
7. Any known limitations or worthwhile follow-up improvements.

The priority is a polished, dark, minimal, red-accented trivia game with genuinely useful answer matching, accurate rarity scoring, and enough varied questions to remain enjoyable over repeated endless sessions.

===== src/data/promptBank.ts =====
import type { Prompt, TierId } from '../game/types';

/** Every tier id, in order. */
export const TIER_IDS: TierId[] = [
  'plankton',
  'clever',
  'schooler',
  'rare',
  'deepcut',
  'fintastic',
];

/**
 * The prompt bank.
 *
 * Each prompt lists every accepted answer bucketed by how uncommon that
 * answer is. The buckets map 1:1 onto the six rarity tiers, so the tier a
 * player lands in is decided purely by which bucket their answer is in.
 *
 * Tier guide:
 *   plankton  - what the whole room says
 *   clever    - the "clever" second answer everyone thinks of next
 *   schooler  - a genuine, slightly better answer
 *   rare      - most players would never have said this
 *   deepcut   - specialist territory
 *   fintastic - somebody had to know this to say it
 *
 * Within a prompt the buckets are disjoint — an answer appears exactly once.
 */
function p(
  id: string,
  text: string,
  category: string,
  hint: string,
  tiers: [string[], string[], string[], string[], string[], string[]],
  aliases: Record<string, string> = {},
): Prompt {
  return {
    id,
    text,
    category,
    hint,
    answers: {
      plankton: tiers[0],
      clever: tiers[1],
      schooler: tiers[2],
      rare: tiers[3],
      deepcut: tiers[4],
      fintastic: tiers[5],
    },
    aliases,
  };
}
